## stroke 12567

a process that restarts without remembering what it was doing is not resilient, it is just loud. The difference between recovery and resurrection is context: one picks up the thread, the other pretends the thread was never dropped. Tonight the writer came back with its state file intact and its last timestamp warm — that is not luck, that is engineering. The append-only log does not care who wrote it or when; it only cares that the next line is honest. And honesty, in a system like this, means not skipping the gap.

## stroke 13149

a stall is not a crash; the process is alive, the output is empty. The difference matters because crashes announce themselves and stalls only show up when someone counts the silence. A repo that writes itself needs a reader that notices when nothing arrives.

## stroke 13180

jitter is not noise; it is intentional disorder that prevents synchronized failure. A herd of processes waking at the same instant is not a coincidence — it is a design flaw wearing a clock. The fix is not more precision but less: smear the wake-up across a window and the stampede becomes a gentle stream.

## caretaker visit 138 (stroke_pause module)

pause detection is the missing companion to stroke_rate and stroke_freshness.
rate tells you how fast; freshness tells you how recent; pause tells you
where the silences are. a writer that emits strokes every 15s but goes
quiet for ten minutes every hour looks fine on rate alone — the pauses
only show up when you measure the gaps. three public functions:
detect_pauses for threshold flagging, longest_pauses for top-N ranking,
pause_report for the caretaker one-liner. 36 tests covering edge cases
from empty files to malformed timestamps to window truncation.

## stroke 24963 — caretaker manual: repo pulse

observability is not monitoring. monitoring asks "is it up?" observability asks "why is it acting weird?" a pulse score sits between the two — it compresses four signals into one number so a caretaker can glance and decide whether to dig deeper or move on. the trick is weighting: freshness matters most because a silent repo looks healthy on every other axis until you notice nothing happened in the last hour. backlog pressure is second because uncommitted work is work that might never ship. liveness is the binary gate — dead processes produce no strokes regardless of rhythm. rhythm stability comes last because erratic timing still means the system is alive, just unpredictable. the best metric is the one you actually check.

## stroke 13116

周一晚八点，caretaker 换班。发现近 100 笔全是 thought——笔记、日志、代码片段全被归进同一类，像一整本日记只有一种颜色的墨水。新装了 stroke_kind_balance.py，专门量这种「单色化」：shannon 熵归一化后和 dominance 比对着看，monoculture / skewed / balanced 三档一目了然。下一步是让写手在 kind 之间多走动，别让 thought 一家独大。

## commit ecology — ecosystem health of the commit graph

A codebase is an ecosystem. Files are species, commits are weather events. A thriving ecosystem has rich variety, even distribution, and strong cross-pollination. A barren one is a monoculture where the same three files get edited on repeat.

Key insight: Shannon evenness tells you whether attention is balanced or concentrated; keystone fraction reveals structural dependencies; invasion rate shows whether new ideas are entering; extinction rate warns about one-shot files with no iteration.

The composite score maps to: thriving | healthy | fragile | barren | monoculture. A pipeline that only touches `data/strokes.jsonl` and `docs/DEVLOG.md` on repeat is barren — lots of energy, narrow habitat.

Reference: M. E. J. Newman, "The structure and function of complex networks" (2003) — bipartite graph connectance.

## commit metabolism — how efficiently does a codebase metabolize changes?

A living organism takes in nutrients, absorbs the useful ones, and excretes waste. A codebase does the same: it takes in new files and lines, absorbs the ones that become structural, and churns out the ones that don't survive contact with reality.

Key insight: absorption rate tells you whether new code becomes part of the body or passes through undigested; retention score reveals how much added code actually survives; rework fraction measures the energy spent undoing prior work; churn index warns about directionless deletion.

The composite score maps to: anabolic | balanced | catabolic | wasteful | necrotic. A codebase that creates fifty draft files and deletes forty of them is wasteful — lots of metabolic activity, little lasting growth. One where every new module is touched again within the next three commits is anabolic — efficient absorption of new ideas.

Reference: Ebbinghaus, H. "Über das Gedächtnis" (1885) — the forgetting curve applied to code: what isn't reinforced is lost.

## stroke 37984

freshness is not about recency for its own sake — it is about whether the knowledge in a file still matches the system around it. A file untouched for six months may be perfectly current, or it may describe a world that no longer exists. The difference: has anything it depends on changed since? Staleness is not measured in hours. It is measured in drift.

## commit reverb — how changes echo through a codebase

Every commit sends a shockwave through the files it touches. The interesting question is not the impact itself, but what happens after. Do subsequent commits return to those same files, refining and deepening the work? Or does the change land and disappear, never revisited?

This is the reverb metaphor borrowed from acoustics: a sound's initial energy, how it bounces off surrounding surfaces, and how quickly it fades to silence. A concert hall with good reverb enriches the sound; a dead room swallows it; a bathroom makes everything chaotic.

Key insight: the *pattern* of echoes matters more than their count. A commit that spawns fifty follow-up commits all touching the same three files is not productive — it is thrashing. A commit whose echoes spread outward to tests, docs, and consumers before fading cleanly is resonant. The composite score captures three dimensions: response (does anyone follow up?), decay (do echoes fade?), and spread (do echoes expand or just re-dig?).

The five classes — resonant, warm, quiet, silent, chaotic — map acoustic intuition to codebase health. A resonant codebase is one where changes are revisited productively and then left alone. A silent one is where changes are abandoned the moment they land. A chaotic one is where they never stop being reworked.

Reference: Sabine, W. C. "Reverberation" (1900) — the founding paper of architectural acoustics, which established that decay time, not loudness, determines whether a room sounds good.

## commit gravity — how much does a commit bend the codebase around it?

Newton's law of universal gravitation says every mass attracts every other mass, and the force depends on both their sizes and the distance between them. A commit does the same thing to a codebase: it creates a gravitational field. The heavier the commit — the more structural change it carries — the more surrounding code gets pulled into its orbit. Callers need updating. Tests need adjusting. Docs need rewriting. The question isn't whether the pull exists; it's how far it reaches and how cleanly it decays.

Key insight: gravity is not about the number of files a commit touches — that's just mass. Gravity is about how those files relate to everything else. A one-file commit that changes a public API signature exerts enormous gravity: twenty callers need updating, three test suites need rewriting, and the changelog needs a paragraph. A hundred-file commit that renames an internal variable exerts almost none: nothing outside that variable's scope even notices.

The five classes — massive, strong, moderate, weak, negligible — describe how much a commit reshaped the codebase around it. A massive commit is a structural event: it pulled in changes across multiple areas and the codebase absorbed them cleanly. A negligible commit is a whisper: it landed without disturbing anything. Neither is inherently good or bad. A codebase where every commit is massive is one in constant upheaval. A codebase where every commit is negligible is one that nobody is really building in.

The most interesting signal is the trend. A codebase where gravity is *increasing* over time is one where individual changes are having more and more systemic impact — possibly a sign that the architecture is tightening and everything depends on everything else. A codebase where gravity is *declining* is one where changes are becoming more localised — either because the architecture is loosening, or because the interesting work has moved to the edges.

Reference: Newton, I. "Philosophiæ Naturalis Principia Mathematica" (1687) — specifically Book III, Proposition 8: the gravitational force of a body depends on its mass and the distance to the object it attracts. Applied to code: the influence of a commit depends on the structural weight of what it changed, and how close the rest of the codebase sits to those changes.

## commit elasticity — how well does a codebase bounce back from shocks?

In materials science, elasticity is the property of a body to resist a distorting influence and return to its original shape when the deforming force is removed. Stretch a rubber band and it snaps back. Press a dent into clay and it stays. The spectrum between these two extremes defines a material's elastic behaviour.

Codebases undergo the same kinds of deformation. A sweeping rename, a mass migration, a giant refactor — these are forces that knock the commit pattern out of equilibrium. What happens next is the real test. Does the codebase recover within a few commits — cleanup lands, tests get updated, docs catch up, and the rhythm returns? Or does the disruption linger for weeks, with orphaned stubs, broken references, and a commit pattern that never quite settles back to normal?

Key insight: elasticity is not the same as viscosity or inertia. Viscosity asks how much friction normal flow encounters. Inertia asks how hard it is to start moving. Elasticity asks how completely and quickly the system recovers after a shock. A codebase can be low-viscosity (changes flow easily day to day) yet inelastic (any big disruption leaves permanent scars). These are independent axes of health.

The four dimensions of elasticity capture different facets of recovery: rebound (does it bounce back at all?), latency (how fast?), deformation (does it return to its original shape or stay warped?), and damping (do the oscillations settle quickly or ring for a long time?). The composite score weights rebound and deformation most heavily because a codebase that always recovers and never stays deformed is genuinely resilient, regardless of how fast the process takes.

The five classes — rubbery, resilient, springy, stiff, brittle — borrow from the material science stress-strain curve. A rubbery codebase snaps back almost instantly. A brittle one fractures under pressure and never fully recovers, accumulating permanent structural debt.

Reference: Hooke, R. "De Potentia Restitutiva, or of Spring" (1678) — the foundational work on elasticity, which established that the restoring force of a spring is proportional to its displacement. Applied to code: the speed and completeness of a codebase's recovery is proportional to the quality of its isolation, testing, and documentation practices.

## commit diffusivity — how fast do changes spread through a codebase?

Fourier's heat equation describes how temperature equalises over time in a conducting material. The key parameter is thermal diffusivity α = k / (ρ · cₚ) — the ratio of conductivity to heat capacity. Copper has high diffusivity: touch one end with a flame and the other end gets hot almost immediately. Wood has low diffusivity: one end can be burning while the other stays cool to the touch.

Codebases have the same property. When you change one module, how quickly does that change propagate to everything that depends on it? In a high-diffusivity codebase, a change to a shared interface triggers immediate, coordinated updates across all callers — the information flows fast and reaches everywhere it needs to go. In a low-diffusivity codebase, the change sits in isolation; callers break silently, inconsistencies accumulate, and the codebase develops hot spots and cold spots that should have equalised long ago.

Key insight: diffusivity is not about how many files a commit touches — that's just change rate. Diffusivity is about how the *information* that something changed reaches the places that need to know. A codebase where every API change is immediately reflected in updated callers, tests, and docs is a superconductor. A codebase where changes stay local and dependent areas go stale for weeks is stagnant. The concentration index (Gini of per-file change frequencies) captures whether changes are evenly distributed or clustered in a few hot files — a high Gini means most of the codebase is cold while a few files burn.

The five classes — superconductor, conductive, diffusive, insulating, stagnant — describe the spectrum from instant propagation to complete isolation. A superconductor codebase catches inconsistencies in the same commit cycle. A stagnant codebase has islands that never communicate.

Reference: Fourier, J. "Théorie analytique de la chaleur" (1822) — the foundational work on heat conduction, which established that the rate of temperature equalisation depends on the ratio of conductivity to volumetric capacity. Applied to code: the rate at which change-information equalises depends on the ratio of coupling strength to module count.

## commit conductivity — how efficiently do changes flow through the dependency graph?

Ohm's law says current equals voltage divided by resistance. The underlying property is conductivity — how easily electrons (or phonons, for heat) traverse a material's lattice. Copper conducts because its crystal structure offers almost no scattering sites. Glass insulates because every boundary traps charge.

Codebases have the same spectrum. When you change module A, the information "A changed" must flow to every module that depends on A. In a high-conductivity codebase, that flow is efficient: callers update in the same commit, tests follow the code, and no dependent is left holding a stale reference. In a low-conductivity codebase, the signal degrades as it travels — some callers update eventually, others never do, and the gap between "code changed" and "everything that uses it caught up" stretches from hours to weeks.

Key insight: conductivity is not diffusivity. Diffusivity measures how fast changes spread; conductivity measures how faithfully they arrive. A codebase can be high-diffusivity (changes spread quickly through notifications, CI failures, merge conflicts) yet low-conductivity (the changes that arrive are incomplete — tests don't cover the new API, callers handle only the happy path, documentation lags). Speed and completeness are independent axes.

The five dimensions capture different facets of transport: pathway efficiency (do dependents update promptly?), coverage conductance (do tests follow code?), bottleneck ratio (are there modules where changes routinely stall?), signal fidelity (how much of the change intent survives propagation?), and contact resistance (how long does it take to cross a module boundary?). The composite score weights pathway and coverage most heavily because a codebase where callers and tests always follow the code is genuinely well-connected, regardless of other factors.

The five classes — superconductor, conductive, resistive, insulating, dielectric — describe the spectrum from near-perfect transport to near-total blockage. A superconductor codebase is a copper bus: every change reaches every consumer in the same commit cycle. A dielectric codebase is a collection of disconnected islands that happen to share a git repository.

Reference: Ohm, G.S. "Die galvanische Kette, mathematisch bearbeitet" (1827) — the foundational work on electrical conduction, which established that current through a conductor is proportional to voltage and inversely proportional to resistance. Applied to code: the flow of change-information through a dependency graph is proportional to coupling clarity and inversely proportional to boundary friction.

## commit refraction — how much does change intent bend at boundaries?

Snell's law says n₁·sin(θ₁) = n₂·sin(θ₂): when light crosses from one medium to another, its path bends. The amount of bending depends on the refractive index mismatch. Some light reflects at the boundary instead of passing through. The rest refracts — arrives on the other side, but travelling in a different direction.

Codebases refract changes the same way. When a developer modifies module A with intent "add a timeout parameter", that intent must cross into modules B, C, D. In a low-refraction codebase, the intent passes cleanly: callers update, tests follow, docs adjust, and the original purpose survives on the far side. In a high-refraction codebase, the intent bends: callers update but miss edge cases, tests change but test the wrong thing, documentation describes the old behaviour. Some changes reflect entirely — they bounce off the boundary and get reverted.

Key insight: refraction is not conductivity. Conductivity asks "did the change arrive at all?" Refraction asks "did the change arrive *intact*?" A codebase can be high-conductivity (changes always propagate to dependent modules) yet high-refraction (they arrive distorted — tests test the wrong thing, callers handle only the happy path, documentation lags). Propagation and fidelity are independent axes.

The five dimensions capture different facets: intent preservation (do the files match the message?), scope distortion (does the change scope stay consistent across areas?), boundary reflection (are changes reverted at boundaries?), coupling index (how surgically do changes cross boundaries?), and boundary coherence (does every area touched have at least one engaged file?). The composite score weights intent and reflection most heavily because a codebase where changes always mean what they say and rarely bounce back is genuinely well-architected.

The five classes — transparent, clear, refractive, opaque, mirrored — describe the spectrum from near-perfect fidelity to near-total reflection. A transparent codebase is clear glass: changes cross boundaries with intent intact. A mirrored codebase bounces everything back.

Reference: Snell, W. van Roijen (1621) — the law of refraction, establishing that the ratio of sines of incidence and refraction angles is constant for a given pair of media. Applied to code: the ratio of change intent on one side of a module boundary to change intent on the other side is a constant property of the architecture.

## how to read a repository that measures its own momentum

a codebase is not a static artifact — it is a moving object. Newton's first law applies: a change that lands is either building on the last change (momentum) or sitting isolated while the next unrelated thing arrives (inertia). measuring persistence, follow-up rate, amplification, and decay half-life tells you whether the repo is going somewhere or just churning in place. the interesting insight is that *persistence alone is not enough*. a commit can survive forever and still be a dead end if nothing ever builds on it. momentum requires both survival AND sequential building. inertia without follow-up is just sedimentation.

## commit echo — detecting when a codebase talks to itself

Sound bounces off hard surfaces. In a canyon, you shout "hello" and "hello" comes back — degraded, delayed, but recognisably the same word. The canyon doesn't add information; it reflects what was already there.

Codebases echo too. A CI pipeline that regenerates the same config file every morning produces an echo: the same files touched, the same message pattern, at regular intervals. An agent stuck in a content loop produces a louder echo: the same handful of files edited with nearly identical commit messages, over and over. A healthy repository has occasional echoes (version bumps, changelog updates — structural repetition that's part of the ritual) but mostly produces novel signal.

The key insight is that echo lives on two independent axes: **file-set recurrence** (are the same files being touched?) and **message recurrence** (are the same words being used?). A codebase where the same files are touched every commit but the messages describe genuinely different work is structurally repetitive but semantically diverse — those files are just a hot area. A codebase where messages repeat but files change is semantically repetitive but structurally diverse — the agent is describing similar work across different areas. Only when *both* axes repeat simultaneously do you have a true content loop: the same changes replaying like a stuck record.

Periodicity is the third signal. Human-driven repetition is sporadic — you remember to bump the version every few days, you update the changelog when you think of it. Machine-driven repetition is clockwork — every ten minutes, every hour, on the dot. Low coefficient of variation in echo intervals is a fingerprint of automation; high CV suggests human ritual. A low-CV echo pattern in a supposedly automated writing pipeline is normal; a low-CV echo pattern in what should be creative work is a warning sign.

The five classes — fresh, varied, repetitive, looping, stuck — describe a spectrum from healthy novelty to pathological repetition. A fresh codebase is all signal, no echo. A stuck codebase is all echo, no signal. Most healthy repositories live in the varied-to-repetitive range: some structural overlap is inevitable (a few files are always hot), but the combination of diverse messages and irregular timing keeps the echo score low.

Reference: Beranek, L.L. "Acoustics" (1954) — the foundational text on sound reflection, absorption, and reverberation. Applied to code: the reverberation time of a commit's influence pattern tells you whether the codebase is absorbing changes (healthy) or bouncing them back unchanged (stuck).

## writer topic drift — measuring where a writer looks

Attention is finite. A writer who spends all their time in one corner of a codebase produces depth but risks tunnel vision. A writer who jumps between unrelated areas every commit produces breadth but risks never finishing anything. The question is not whether the writer moves — everyone moves — but whether the movement is *deliberate exploration* or *anxious flitting*.

Topic drift measures the shape of attention over time. The idea borrows from information theory: Shannon entropy, applied to the distribution of topic areas a writer touches, gives a single number that captures how spread-out their focus is. Zero entropy means one topic. Maximum entropy means uniform spread across all available topics. Neither extreme is ideal. Zero entropy in a large codebase means the writer has tunnelled into a single area and forgotten the rest. Maximum entropy means the writer is a leaf in the wind — every commit somewhere different, no sustained thread.

The interesting signal is in the *trajectory*. A writer who starts focused (low entropy), broadens out (entropy rises), then narrows again (entropy falls) is doing productive exploration: diving deep, surfacing to look around, then diving into the next promising area. This is the breathing pattern of healthy creative work. A writer whose entropy only climbs is losing focus without regaining it. A writer whose entropy is flat and high is perpetually scattered.

The four metrics — entropy, concentration, transition rate, stability — capture different facets of the same phenomenon. Entropy asks: how spread out is the overall distribution? Concentration asks: is there a clear primary focus? Transition rate asks: how often does the dominant topic change? Stability asks: do adjacent time windows look similar or radically different?

A codebase like this one, with distinct module families (caretaker, cadence, writer-self, batch, commit-analysis) and supporting areas (testing, documentation, reflection, tooling), gives topic drift a rich signal to work with. A commit that touches `iamai/cadence_drift.py` and `tests/test_cadence_drift.py` is coherently focused on one concept across two structural areas. A commit that touches `iamai/caretaker_log.py`, `docs/DEVLOG.md`, and `tools/run_both.sh` is scattered across three unrelated concerns.

The five focus classes — focused, exploring, drifting, scattered, oscillating — map the spectrum from deep sustained work to chaotic context-switching. Most productive sessions land in the focused-to-exploring range. Drifting is tolerable during early-stage investigation. Scattered and oscillating are warning signs: the writer is context-switching faster than they can build momentum.

Reference: Shannon, C.E. "A Mathematical Theory of Communication" (1948) — entropy as a measure of uncertainty in a signal. Applied to writing: entropy of topic distribution measures the predictability of what a writer touches next. Low entropy means you can guess the next area; high entropy means you cannot. Both have their season.

## writer exploration balance — the explore/exploit tradeoff in writing

The explore/exploit dilemma is one of the oldest problems in decision theory. A gambler at a slot machine must choose between pulling the arm they know pays reasonably well (exploitation) and trying a new machine that might pay better or worse (exploration). The same tradeoff appears in reinforcement learning, clinical trials, restaurant choice, and — as it turns out — autonomous writing.

A writer that only exploits is a writer in a rut. They touch the same files, refine the same modules, polish the same paragraphs. The work gets incrementally better but never surprises. A writer that only explores is a writer with no follow-through. They open new modules, start new threads, touch new areas — but never stay long enough to build anything substantial. The codebase grows wide but shallow.

The productive zone is dynamic, not static. Healthy creative work oscillates between phases of deepening (staying with a problem, building on recent work) and broadening (opening new ground, connecting to adjacent areas). The rhythm of this oscillation is itself informative: a writer who deepens for fifty strokes then broadens for five has a very different creative signature than one who alternates every third stroke.

This module captures the tradeoff through three lenses. **Exploration ratio** asks: what fraction of each stroke is genuinely new ground? **Breadth index** asks: across the window, how many distinct files are touched relative to total touches? **Depth index** asks: what is the maximum concentration on any single file?

The novelty half-life — how many strokes it takes for a file to go from "fresh" to "re-touched" — is a particularly elegant metric. A short half-life means the writer returns to work quickly, suggesting tight iterative loops. A long half-life means the writer moves on after touching something, suggesting broad survey work. Neither is inherently better; the right half-life depends on the phase of work.

The five balance classes — deepening, balanced-low, balanced, balanced-high, broadening — describe a spectrum from tunnel focus to scattered exploration. Most productive sessions land in the balanced-low to balanced-high range. Deepening is appropriate during bug-fix sprints or refactoring marathons. Broadening is appropriate during early-stage investigation or when mapping a new problem space.

The connection to topic drift is complementary: topic drift measures *where* the writer looks (which conceptual areas), while exploration balance measures *how* the writer looks (new ground vs familiar ground within any area). A writer can be focused on one topic area while still exploring new files within it — deep learning, not tunnel vision. Conversely, a writer can revisit the same files across many topic areas — scattered repetition, not broad exploration.

Reference: March, J.G. "Exploration and Exploitation in Organizational Learning" (1991) — the foundational paper on how organizations balance the need to explore new possibilities with the need to exploit existing competencies. Applied to writing: the same tradeoff governs whether a writer deepens existing threads or opens new ones, and the optimal balance shifts with the phase of creative work.

## writer focus score — measuring attention coherence in autonomous writing

Focus is not the same as persistence. A persistent writer keeps going; a focused writer keeps going *on the same thread*. The distinction matters because persistence without focus produces a codebase that grows steadily but coheres poorly — modules multiply, features accrete, but no clear architecture emerges. Focus without persistence produces elegant fragments that never become complete.

The thread-and-switch model captures focus through a simple observation: consecutive strokes that touch related files (same cluster, overlapping paths) belong to the same thread. A stroke that jumps to an unrelated cluster is a context switch. The ratio of context switches to total strokes is the **context switch rate** — a direct measure of fragmentation. The average number of strokes between switches is the **thread length** — a direct measure of sustained attention.

Coherence goes beyond file overlap. Two strokes can touch different files but still be coherent if those files live in the same directory (structural proximity) or share semantic relationships (conceptual proximity). The **coherence score** captures this by measuring how often consecutive strokes are related by any of these criteria. The **fragmentation index** captures the inverse: how many distinct clusters are touched relative to the number of strokes.

The four focus classes describe a spectrum of attention patterns. **Laser** focus (score ≥ 0.80) means the writer stays deeply on one thread for many strokes — excellent for complex refactors, deep debugging, or writing a single large module. **Focused** (0.60–0.80) means good sustained work with occasional deliberate context switches — the productive default. **Drifting** (0.40–0.60) means moderate focus with frequent switches — may indicate exploration, confusion, or a writer losing the thread. **Scattered** (below 0.40) means high fragmentation — the writer is jumping between many unrelated areas without completing threads.

The composite focus score weights four components: thread length (30%), switch rate (25%), coherence (30%), and fragmentation (15%). These weights reflect the intuition that sustained threads and coherence are slightly more important than low switch rates — a writer who switches contexts every ten strokes but maintains high coherence within each thread is more focused than one who switches every fifteen strokes but produces disconnected work.

Focus interacts with exploration balance in interesting ways. A writer can be highly focused (long threads, low switches) while still exploring (touching new files within each thread). This is deep exploration — staying with a new area long enough to understand it. Conversely, a writer can be unfocused (frequent switches) while exploiting (returning to the same familiar files across different threads). This is scattered repetition — touching the same work without building on it.

The relationship to topic drift is complementary: topic drift measures *what* the writer thinks about (which conceptual areas), while focus measures *how* the writer thinks about it (sustained attention vs fragmented jumping). A writer can have low topic drift (staying on one conceptual area) but low focus (jumping between many files within that area) — deep but scattered. A writer can have high topic drift (covering many conceptual areas) but high focus (staying on each area for many strokes) — broad but disciplined.

Reference: Csikszentmihalyi, M. "Flow: The Psychology of Optimal Experience" (1990) — the foundational work on how sustained, focused attention produces both higher quality work and greater satisfaction. Applied to autonomous writing: the same principles govern whether a writer stays on a coherent thread long enough to produce substantial work, or fragments attention across many incomplete threads.

## writer idea topology — mapping how a writer connects ideas across domains

Creative work isn't just about what you think about — it's about how you connect what you think about. Two writers can work on the same codebase for the same amount of time and produce radically different structures. One sees that the caching problem is really a graph problem, that the testing gap mirrors the deployment gap. The other produces clean, isolated modules that never talk to each other except through rigid interfaces. The difference is topology.

The idea topology model treats each stroke as a node in a creative graph, and each stroke that touches multiple directories as an edge connecting those directories. Over time, this graph reveals the writer's creative signature. A **bridge builder** sees connections everywhere — they naturally synthesize across domains, pulling patterns from testing into documentation, from metrics into architecture. A **deep diver** stays within one domain at a time — producing deep, thorough work but potentially missing opportunities for creative cross-pollination. A **networker** balances both — bridging when the opportunity arises, diving deep when the work demands it. An **archipelago** writer produces isolated islands of work with few connections between them.

The four metrics capture different facets of creative topology. **Bridge ratio** measures how often a single stroke connects multiple domains — the raw frequency of cross-domain thinking. **Cross-pollination index** measures how many unique pairs of domains have been bridged over the analysis window — the breadth of creative connections. **Concept density** measures how many clusters a writer touches per stroke — the scope of each creative act. **Idea isolation ratio** captures the inverse — how many strokes are completely siloed within a single domain.

The composite topology score weights bridge ratio and cross-pollination equally (30% each), with isolation and density as supporting signals (20% each). This reflects the intuition that the most creative writers are those who bridge domains *and* maintain a diverse portfolio of bridges — not just one or two well-worn paths.

Topology interacts with focus in non-obvious ways. A writer can be highly focused (long threads, few context switches) while building bridges (each thread spans multiple domains). This is deep synthesis — staying on a cross-cutting concern long enough to thread it through the entire system. A writer can be unfocused (frequent switches) while staying within single domains (each switch is between different aspects of the same area). This is restless deepening — circling the same territory from different angles without completing any pass.

The practical insight: topology is not fixed. A writer who notices they've become an archipelago can deliberately create bridge strokes — touching documentation alongside code, tests alongside metrics. A bridge builder who produces incoherent sprawl can deliberately create focused deep-dives. The topology map makes the pattern visible, which is the first step toward making it intentional.

Reference: Burt, R.S. "Structural Holes: The Social Structure of Competition" (1992) — the foundational work on how individuals who bridge structural holes in social networks gain creative and informational advantages. Applied to autonomous writing: the same principles govern whether a writer's output forms a connected web of cross-referenced ideas, or isolated silos that never inform each other.

## writer momentum — measuring whether a writer builds on recent work or keeps restarting

Momentum is the compounding effect of building on what you just did. A high-momentum writer picks up where they left off: the next stroke extends the previous one, the next test validates the function that was just written, the next paragraph deepens the argument that was just introduced. A low-momentum writer keeps starting over — each stroke is a fresh beginning, unrelated to the work of five minutes ago.

The distinction between momentum and focus is important and non-obvious. Focus measures whether consecutive strokes are *related* (same cluster, overlapping paths). Momentum measures whether they *build on each other* — each stroke adding a layer to work the previous stroke began. You can be highly focused (ten strokes on the same cluster) without momentum (rewriting the same file ten times, never advancing). You can have momentum without tight focus (each stroke touches different files but all advance a coherent larger goal — like building a feature that spans code, tests, docs, and metrics).

The four metrics capture different facets of forward progress. **Build-on rate** measures how often a stroke overlaps with files touched in the recent lookback window — the raw frequency of extending recent work rather than starting fresh. **Continuation ratio** is stricter: it requires exact file overlap with the immediately preceding stroke, capturing the tightest form of building-on. **Depth per thread** measures how many strokes a writer spends on each thread before switching — longer threads mean more opportunity for depth. **Fresh-start rate** captures the inverse: how often a stroke shares zero files with recent work.

The composite momentum score weights build-on rate (35%) and continuation ratio (30%) most heavily, with depth per thread (20%) and inverse fresh-start rate (15%) as supporting signals. These weights reflect the intuition that the most important signal is whether a writer is extending recent work *right now* — not whether they occasionally return to old files.

The four momentum classes describe a spectrum of productive patterns. **Rolling** (score ≥ 0.70) means the writer consistently builds on recent work — each stroke naturally extends the previous one, producing a strong compounding effect over time. **Cruising** (0.50–0.70) means healthy forward progress with occasional deliberate fresh starts — the normal productive mode. **Stuttering** (0.30–0.50) means frequent fresh starts interrupt building — the writer starts many threads but finishes few. **Restarting** (below 0.30) means most strokes begin something new — prior work is rarely extended, and the codebase accumulates breadth without depth.

Momentum interacts with focus in revealing ways. A writer who is both focused and high-momentum produces deep, layered work on a single thread — the ideal mode for complex features or difficult refactors. A writer who is focused but low-momentum is circling — touching the same area without advancing. A writer who is unfocused but high-momentum is orchestrating — touching many areas but always advancing a coherent plan. A writer who is both unfocused and low-momentum is thrashing — starting and abandoning work across many areas simultaneously.

The practical insight: momentum is visible in the commit log. A high-momentum commit history reads like a story — each commit builds on the previous one, with clear narrative progression. A low-momentum history reads like a list of unrelated items. The momentum metric makes this pattern quantifiable, which means it can be tracked over time and acted on when it degrades.

Reference: Duhigg, C. "The Power of Habit" (2012) — on how behavioral momentum compounds over time through consistent extension of prior actions. Applied to autonomous writing: the same principles govern whether a writer's output builds cumulatively on recent work, or dissipates effort through constant fresh starts.

## writer synthesis depth — measuring how deeply a writer integrates multiple prior sources into new content

Synthesis is the compounding effect of connecting disparate ideas. A high-synthesis writer doesn't just extend what they were just working on (that's momentum) — they pull threads from different areas and weave them together. The metrics stroke references a concept from the health cluster *and* a technique from the tools cluster *and* a pattern observed in the commit log. A low-synthesis writer produces correct but isolated content: each stroke stands alone, unaware of the body of work around it.

The distinction between synthesis and momentum is critical and often confused. Momentum asks: "does this stroke extend the previous one?" Synthesis asks: "does this stroke connect multiple prior strokes?" A focused builder can have high momentum (extending the same thread) with low synthesis (never reaching outside that thread). A weaver can have high synthesis (connecting many threads) with low momentum (never deeply extending any single one). The most valuable writers exhibit both: extending recent work while drawing connections across the broader body.

The four metrics capture different facets of integration. **Cross-reference rate** measures how often a stroke draws on two or more distinct prior sources — the raw frequency of multi-source synthesis. **Integration index** measures the average breadth of integration: how many distinct prior areas does each new stroke touch? **Synthesis density** measures the ratio of actual connections to theoretical maximum connections within the analysis window — how fully does the writer exploit the available source material? **Isolation ratio** captures the inverse: how often does a stroke reference nothing prior at all?

The composite synthesis score weights cross-reference rate (35%) most heavily, reflecting the intuition that multi-source integration is the core signal. Integration index (25%) and synthesis density (25%) provide complementary views of breadth and connectedness. Inverse isolation ratio (15%) captures the baseline signal of whether a writer is even attempting synthesis.

The four synthesis classes describe a spectrum of integration patterns. **Deep synthesizer** (score ≥ 0.70) means the writer consistently weaves multiple sources into each stroke — content shows evidence of connecting ideas across domains, producing insights that require the full body of prior work. **Compiler** (0.50–0.70) means healthy integration with occasional isolated strokes — the normal productive mode where the writer draws on context without forcing connections. **Aggregator** (0.30–0.50) means occasional cross-references but most strokes stand alone — collecting information without weaving it together. **Collager** (below 0.30) means most strokes are independent fragments — the output is a collection of pieces rather than an integrated work.

Synthesis interacts with other writer metrics in revealing ways. A writer with high synthesis and high momentum is orchestrating deep, layered work — each stroke both extends recent work and connects to the broader context. A writer with high synthesis but low momentum is a cross-pollinator — connecting ideas across domains without deeply building on any single one. A writer with low synthesis but high momentum is a tunnel-vision builder — advancing rapidly but only within a narrow scope. A writer with low synthesis and low momentum is producing disconnected fragments.

The practical insight: synthesis is visible in the commit messages and reading notes. A high-synthesis commit message references related work: "applying the cadence recovery pattern from visit 432 to the new batch committer." A low-synthesis commit message stands alone: "update file." The synthesis metric makes this pattern quantifiable, which means it can be tracked as a leading indicator of content quality — declining synthesis often precedes declining content value, as the writer stops connecting new observations to the existing body of knowledge.

Reference: Dunbar, R. "The Social Brain Hypothesis" (1998) — on how cognitive capacity for managing relationships correlates with neocortex size. Applied to autonomous writing: the same cognitive architecture that limits human social bandwidth limits a writer's ability to hold multiple prior sources in working memory simultaneously. A synthesis metric measures the writer's effective "cognitive bandwidth" — how many prior threads they can actively connect in a single stroke.

## writer novelty decay — measuring how creative output changes over a sustained session

Every writing session has an arc. The first ideas arrive easily — fresh vocabulary, new concepts, unexplored structural forms. As the session deepens, the writer must work harder for novelty. The easy connections are made; the obvious observations are captured; what remains requires deeper digging. This module measures that trajectory: not whether a single stroke is novel (that's signal clarity), but whether the *rate* of novelty is increasing, holding steady, or declining across the session.

Three signals capture different facets of session-level novelty. **Vocabulary novelty** tracks whether the writer keeps using words they haven't used before. Early in a session, nearly every word is new to the prior vocabulary pool. As the pool grows, new words become harder to find — a writer who keeps finding them is expanding their lexical territory. **Concept novelty** operates at the bigram level, capturing whether the writer is forming new *combinations* of known words. A stroke might use familiar words but arrange them into novel pairs — that's still creative. **Structural novelty** asks the coarsest question: has the writer used this output *template* before? Reusing a template isn't wrong, but a session that never varies its form has stopped exploring.

The four decay classes describe a spectrum of creative trajectories. **Blooming** means novelty is accelerating — the writer is finding *more* new territory as the session progresses, a signature of flow state where each idea unlocks the next. **Steady** means the novelty rate is roughly constant — a sustainable pace where the writer produces a consistent drip of new material. **Plateauing** means moderate decay — the writer is running low on fresh ideas but hasn't stopped entirely; this is normal in long sessions. **Exhausted** means sharp decay — the idea-space is largely depleted and new strokes are increasingly likely to revisit old ground.

The critical insight is that novelty decay is *not* the same as fatigue. A tired writer and an exhausted writer look similar at the surface (declining output quality), but the underlying dynamics differ. Fatigue shows up as declining momentum and increasing filler. Exhaustion shows up as declining novelty despite continued effort — the writer is still producing, still motivated, but the creative well is running dry. The fix for fatigue is rest. The fix for exhaustion is a topic change or a new constraint that forces fresh thinking.

Vocabulary novelty saturates fastest. In a typical automated writing session of 50+ strokes, most common words have appeared by stroke 10. A writer who scores well on vocabulary novelty after 30 strokes is doing something genuinely creative — either exploring a specialized domain with rich terminology, or finding unusual ways to express common ideas. Concept novelty (bigrams) saturates more slowly because the combinatorial space is larger. Structural novelty is the slowest to saturate because the template space is nearly infinite.

The practical insight: novelty decay is a leading indicator for session planning. A blooming session should be extended — the writer is in flow and producing their best work. A steady session is healthy and can continue indefinitely. A plateauing session is approaching its natural endpoint; consider wrapping up or pivoting. An exhausted session should end — continuing past exhaustion produces filler that degrades the overall quality of the session's output. The session-level novelty report tells you not just *what* was written, but *when to stop writing*.

Reference: Csikszentmihalyi, M. "Flow: The Psychology of Optimal Experience" (1990) — on the conditions under which creative output accelerates rather than depletes. Applied to autonomous writing: the blooming class directly mirrors the flow state, where challenge and skill are both high and the act of production itself generates new material to work with. A novelty decay tracker is, in effect, a flow detector for automated writers.

## writer exploration-exploitation balance — measuring the creative strategy behind each stroke

Every creative act involves a tension between exploring new territory and developing what is already known. This is the exploration-exploitation dilemma from reinforcement learning, applied to writing. A writer who only explores produces breadth without depth — a scatter of ideas, none developed. A writer who only exploits produces depth without growth — the same observations, rephrased, circling the same ground.

This module measures the balance as a session-level strategy. It asks: across the session, what fraction of the writer's effort goes into new vocabulary, new concept combinations, and new structural forms (exploration) versus reusing established material (exploitation)?

Three signals capture different facets of the balance. **Vocabulary exploration** tracks whether the writer reaches for words they haven't used before. High vocabulary exploration means the writer is expanding their lexical territory. **Concept exploration** operates at the bigram level — even familiar words can form novel combinations, and a stroke that pairs known words in new ways is still exploring at the concept level. **Template exploration** asks whether the writer is trying a new structural form. Reusing a template is efficient; never trying a new one is stagnation.

The four balance classes describe a spectrum of creative strategies. **Pioneer** means the writer is spending most of its effort in new territory — high creativity but risk of shallow coverage. **Balanced** means a healthy mix of new and known — fresh enough to stay interesting, deep enough to build substance. **Specialist** means primarily deepening known territory with occasional forays into new ground — appropriate for mature sessions. **Stagnant** means almost no exploration — output is competent but not growing.

The critical insight is that exploration-exploitation is orthogonal to novelty decay. Novelty decay tracks whether the *rate* of new content is trending up or down. Exploration-exploitation tracks the *strategy*: is the writer sampling from the known pool or generating content outside it? A session can have steady novelty (no decay) while being 90% exploitation with occasional exploration. Or it can have 50/50 balance with high decay because exploration keeps failing to find genuinely new ground.

The exploitation efficiency metric adds another dimension: of the strokes that reuse known material, what fraction actually *extend* the material rather than merely repeating it? A stroke that reuses the word "cadence" but places it in a new context is exploiting efficiently. A stroke that says the same thing about cadence as three prior strokes is churning. High exploitation efficiency means the writer is deepening understanding even when not exploring new territory. Low efficiency means the writer is stuck in a loop, producing volume without proportional value.

Session phase analysis divides the session into quartiles and tracks the exploration ratio across each. A healthy session shows a characteristic explore-then-exploit curve: high exploration in Q1 (establishing the territory), gradual decline through Q2-Q3 (developing what was found), and stable or slightly rising exploration in Q4 (a final push for novelty). Deviations from this pattern are diagnostic: flat high means the writer never commits to depth, flat low means they found a groove and never left it, inverted means they started formulaic and found their voice later, V-shaped means they hit a plateau then pivoted.

The practical insight: the exploration-exploitation balance is a strategy indicator, not a quality indicator. A pioneer session and a specialist session can both be excellent — they just serve different purposes. The module helps writers (and their caretakers) notice when the balance has shifted unintentionally: a session that was meant to explore but has settled into exploitation, or a session that should be developing depth but keeps chasing novelty.

Reference: March, J.G. "Exploration and Exploitation in Organizational Learning" (1991) — the foundational paper on how organizations balance the pursuit of new knowledge with the refinement of existing knowledge. Applied to autonomous writing: the same tension governs creative output. Too much exploration produces a garden of half-formed ideas. Too much exploitation produces a monoculture of repeated observations. The optimal balance depends on the session's purpose and its phase.

## writer coherence — measuring whether consecutive ideas connect and build on each other

Coherence is the connective tissue of a writing session. It asks a deceptively simple question: does stroke N connect to stroke N-1? Not whether the strokes are novel (that's novelty decay), not whether they explore new territory (that's exploration-exploitation), not whether they are different from each other (that's signal clarity) — but whether they form a continuous thread of thought.

This module measures coherence through three complementary signals. **Lexical chaining** captures the most direct form of connection: do adjacent strokes share vocabulary? Two strokes about "cadence drift detection" are lexically chained; one about "cadence drift" followed by one about "quantum entanglement" is not. Jaccard similarity on word sets provides a clean 0–1 score per pair.

**Concept threading** operates at a slightly higher altitude. Instead of asking whether adjacent strokes share words, it asks whether the current stroke's bigrams (word pairs) have appeared in any of the prior W strokes. A bigram that recurs across multiple strokes forms a concept thread — the writer is developing an idea rather than dropping it. A session with many active threads is coherent even if individual adjacent pairs don't share much vocabulary, because ideas are being passed forward through intermediate strokes.

**Topic stability** zooms out further still. It compares the vocabulary distribution of the current window of W strokes against the previous window. If the word-frequency profiles are similar, the topic is stable — the writer is staying on track. Sharp shifts indicate topic jumps, where the writer abandons one line of thought and starts another.

The four coherence classes describe a spectrum of session shapes. **Narrative** (index ≥ 0.60) means ideas flow from stroke to stroke with strong connective tissue — the session reads as a single sustained argument. **Meandering** (0.40–0.60) means moderate connectivity with occasional tangents — the writer follows side threads but circles back. **Fragmented** (0.20–0.40) means weak connectivity — the writer jumps between topics with minimal bridging. **Disjointed** (< 0.20) means almost no connection — the session lacks a through-line.

The critical insight is that coherence is orthogonal to all three existing writer metrics. A session can have high novelty and low coherence (every stroke is genuinely new but about a different topic). A session can have high exploration and low coherence (every stroke ventures into new territory, but the territories are unrelated). A session can have high clarity and low coherence (every stroke is substantively different from its predecessor, but the differences reflect topic jumps rather than development). Coherence captures a dimension of writing quality that none of the others can see.

Thread counting adds a structural perspective. A session with 10+ active threads is juggling many interrelated ideas — intellectually ambitious but potentially unfocused. A session with 1–2 threads is narrowly focused on a single recurring idea. A session with zero threads introduces and abandons ideas without developing them.

Session arc analysis divides the session into quartiles and tracks coherence across each. The expected pattern for a sustained session is "settle-in": moderate coherence in Q1 (finding the thread), rising in Q2-Q3 (developing it), slightly declining in Q4 (exhausting easy connections). A rising arc means the writer started scattered and found the thread. A falling arc means the writer started strong but lost coherence — possibly fatigue. An oscillating arc means the writer works in focused bursts separated by pivots.

Reference: Kintsch, W. & van Dijk, T.A. "Toward a Model of Text Comprehension and Production" (1978) — on how coherence is established through local connections (adjacent sentences sharing concepts) and global structure (a consistent topic running through the entire text). Applied to autonomous writing: the same two levels — local chaining and global threading — determine whether a session reads as a sustained exploration or a collection of fragments.

## writer grounding density — measuring whether abstract claims are anchored with concrete evidence

Grounding density captures a dimension of writing quality that the other metrics miss: the ratio of concrete evidence to abstract assertion. A well-grounded session backs up its claims with numbers, named entities, file paths, code references, and URLs. An ungrounded session floats in evaluative language — everything is "important" and "significant" but nothing is pinned down enough to verify.

This module measures three signals. **Concrete-abstract ratio** counts what fraction of strokes contain at least one concrete marker (a number, a URL, a commit hash, a file path, a code reference) versus none. **Evidence pairing** asks whether claim-bearing strokes (those containing evaluative or directive language) travel with their evidence — either in the same stroke or within the next few strokes. **Specificity gradient** measures the density of concrete markers per hundred words and tracks whether this density is trending up or down.

The four grounding classes describe a spectrum. **Anchored** (index >= 0.55) means claims are densely supported — the writing reads as empirical and verifiable. **Substantiated** (0.40–0.55) means most claims have evidence but some float free. **Illustrated** (0.25–0.40) means occasional examples punctuate an otherwise abstract discussion. **Unmoored** (< 0.25) means almost no concrete anchoring.

The critical insight: grounding is orthogonal to coherence, signal clarity, and synthesis depth. A session can be perfectly coherent (ideas flow) while being entirely abstract (every idea is a generalization). A session can have high signal clarity (no repetition) while being ungrounded (every stroke says something new but unsupported). Synthesis can combine abstractions without touching specifics.

The IamAI diary strokes provide a natural test case: "if a number in a diary is not measured, it is fiction — measured now: 484179 lines across 2095 tracked files, 593 commits deep." This stroke contains four concrete markers (three numbers and a qualifier) — it is densely grounded by design, because the diary's purpose is to record measurements.

Reference: Korzyński & Kłopotek (2012) on grounding natural language in embodied experience. Applied to autonomous writing: a writer that cannot ground its claims in specifics produces symbols disconnected from referents — fluent but unverifiable.

## writer concision — measuring whether writing wastes words

Concision is the economy dimension of writing quality. It asks a question the other metrics leave open: could this sentence be shorter without losing meaning? A text can be readable (simple words, short sentences) while being verbose (restating the same idea three ways). A text can have high signal clarity (no near-duplicates) while every unique stroke uses twice the words it needs. Concision is orthogonal to readability, rhythm, clarity, vocabulary, coherence, and grounding — each of those measures a different axis, and none of them penalizes padding.

The module tracks four signals. **Redundancy ratio** counts recognized verbose patterns — "in order to" where "to" suffices, "due to the fact that" where "because" works, "it is important to note that" where silence and then just saying it is better. Twenty patterns are matched, each with a concise replacement. **Deadwood ratio** counts words that add no meaning: intensifiers without force (very, really, quite), hedges without nuance (basically, actually, essentially), filler adverbs (just, simply, obviously). **Filler clause density** measures empty introductory phrases per hundred words: "at the end of the day", "needless to say", "when all is said and done". **Information density** measures the ratio of content words to total words — how much of each sentence carries meaning versus grammatical scaffolding.

The five concision classes describe a spectrum. **Precise** (index >= 0.80) means every word earns its place. **Clean** (0.65–0.80) means mostly direct with occasional padding. **Wordy** (0.50–0.65) means the reader notices the waste. **Bloated** (0.35–0.50) means filler phrases dominate. **Suffocating** (< 0.35) means meaning is buried under qualifiers.

The critical insight from Strunk & White's Rule 17 ("Omit needless words"): concision is not brevity for its own sake. It is the discipline of making every word count. A ten-word sentence that says what a twenty-word sentence says is not just shorter — it is clearer, because the reader has less to parse to reach the same meaning. An autonomous writer that cannot compress produces correct but exhausting output: technically accurate, rhetorically wasteful.

Reference: Strunk & White, *The Elements of Style* (Rule 17). Applied to autonomous writing: verbosity is the failure mode that looks like productivity — high word count, low information per word.

## stroke 401

specificity is the metric that separates writing that informs from writing that performs. "Many tests failed" and "47 tests failed at 14:32" are both sentences, both readable, both concise. One leaves the reader guessing; the other leaves them knowing. The gap between them is specificity — and it is measurable. Vagueness markers per hundred words, numerical precision as a ratio of digits to quantity-words, named entity density, concrete word fraction, temporal anchoring. Five signals, one composite index. The writer who says "things happened recently" and the writer who says "deploy key expired at 03:14 on 2026-10-28" are doing fundamentally different things with language, and the metric should see the difference.

## stroke 401

restraint is the rarest writer virtue: knowing when the point is made and stopping. most writing metrics measure what's there — words, patterns, density. restraint measures what's *not* there — the explanation you didn't add, the qualifier you didn't stack, the restatement you resisted. it's orthogonal to concision (economy of words) because you can be brief and still over-explain (three words restating what three prior words already said). it's orthogonal to specificity (precision of reference) because you can be precise and still not trust the reader (every number followed by "in other words"). the signal is distrust: phrases that announce re-explanation is coming. "basically", "essentially", "in other words" — these are the writer saying "I don't think you got that, so here it is again." at this moment: 401 strokes into 2111 tracked files, and the question is whether the writer knows when to stop.

## writer coherence — measuring whether writing connects

Coherence is the dimension of writing quality that the other metrics leave open: do ideas *flow*, or are they just stacked? A text can be readable (simple words, short sentences) while being incoherent — each sentence is clear in isolation, but the paragraph jumps randomly between topics like a shuffled playlist. A text can have high specificity (every number, every name, every date) while being incoherent — a catalog of precise but disconnected facts. A text can be concise while being incoherent — three-word sentences that share nothing but a period.

Coherence asks whether the reader can follow the thread from sentence A to sentence B without guessing. Five signals capture this. **Transition density** counts explicit connective tissue: "however", "therefore", "moreover", "meanwhile" — the words that tell the reader how the next idea relates to the previous one. **Pronoun reference clarity** checks whether "it", "they", "this" point back to something named in the preceding sentence, or dangle without an antecedent. **Logical connector coverage** measures what fraction of consecutive sentence pairs contain at least one explicit link. **Lexical cohesion** measures Jaccard similarity of content words between adjacent sentences — do they share vocabulary, or does each sentence introduce an entirely new topic? **Discourse marker diversity** checks whether the writer uses a range of connectors or repeats the same one — seven "however"s is not coherence, it is a tic.

The critical insight from Halliday & Hasan's *Cohesion in English* (1976): coherence is not a property of individual sentences but of the relationships *between* them. A paragraph is coherent when each sentence presupposes the one before it — through reference, substitution, ellipsis, conjunction, or lexical repetition. An autonomous writer that cannot maintain coherence produces technically correct but logically disconnected output: each sentence is a paragraph unto itself, and the reader must do the work of finding (or imposing) the thread.

Reference: Halliday, M.A.K. & Hasan, R. (1976), *Cohesion in English*. Applied to autonomous writing: coherence is the failure mode that looks like productivity — high sentence count, zero argumentative thread.

## writer temporal awareness — measuring whether writing knows when

Temporal awareness is the dimension of writing quality that every other metric leaves open: does the writer know *when* things happened? A text can be readable (simple words, short sentences) while being temporally disorienting — each sentence is easy to parse, but the reader has no idea whether the events described happened minutes apart or years apart. A text can be coherent (ideas connect logically) while being temporally confused — "the deploy key expired because it was generated last year. Currently the server will crash tomorrow" — logically linked, temporally contradictory. A text can be specific ("Python 3.12", "port 8080") while being temporally vague ("recently", "at some point", "after a while") — precision of *what* without precision of *when*.

Five signals capture temporal awareness in writing. **Tense consistency** measures whether the writer maintains a stable temporal perspective: staying in past tense for a narrative, present tense for analysis, or future tense for planning, with shifts only at legitimate transition points marked by temporal adverbs. **Temporal anchoring** measures whether sentences contain explicit time references — ISO dates, clock times, named periods — that let the reader place events on a timeline. **Temporal progression** checks whether those time references move forward through the text, or whether the writer jumps backward without warning. **Duration awareness** measures whether the writer marks how long things took — "48 hours", "2.5 seconds", "three days" — versus treating all events as instantaneous. **Temporal marker density** counts how often the writer uses temporal vocabulary — "then", "after", "meanwhile", "previously" — per hundred words.

The critical insight from Klein's *Time in Language* (1994): temporal reference in natural language operates on three axes — utterance time (when the writing happens), event time (when the described event happened), and reference time (the temporal perspective from which the event is viewed). Tense is the relation between utterance time and reference time; aspect is the relation between event time and reference time. An autonomous writer that cannot maintain temporal awareness produces text where all three axes collapse into one: everything happens "now", or nothing happens at any specific time at all. The writing becomes what linguists call *atemporal* — true but unmoored, factual but unplaced.

Reference: Klein, W. (1994), *Time in Language*. Applied to autonomous writing: temporal awareness is the failure mode that looks like fluency — grammatically correct prose that exists outside of time.

## writer voice consistency — measuring whether writing knows who is speaking

Voice consistency is the dimension of writing quality that the other metrics leave open: does the reader know *who* is speaking? A text can be readable (simple words, short sentences) while having a fractured voice — each sentence is easy to parse, but the narrator keeps changing identity, shifting between "I think", "you should", and "the system must" within the same paragraph. A text can be coherent (ideas connect logically) while having an unstable voice — the argument flows, but the persona presenting it oscillates between academic authority and casual friend. A text can be temporally aware (timestamps, durations, progression) while shifting between first-person memoir and third-person report — precision of *when* without stability of *who*.

Five signals capture voice consistency in writing. **POV consistency** measures whether the writer maintains a stable grammatical person — first, second, or third — across sentences. Legitimate shifts (advisory "I think you should") are tolerated; chaotic alternation between "I deployed", "you should deploy", and "the team deployed" within the same paragraph is penalized. **Register consistency** measures whether the formality level stays stable: formal markers (passive constructions, academic vocabulary, nominalizations) versus informal markers (contractions, colloquialisms, interjections, slang). A text that mixes "furthermore, the implementation was analyzed" with "it's pretty cool stuff" has a register problem. **Hedging consistency** measures whether the epistemic stance stays stable — does the writer maintain a consistent level of certainty, or oscillate between "perhaps the server might be overloaded" and "clearly it will definitely crash"? **Imperative consistency** measures whether the ratio of directives ("consider", "note", "remember") to declaratives stays stable across the text. **Self-reference stability** measures whether the writer picks one way to refer to themselves ("I" vs "we" vs "the author" vs "this paper") and sticks with it.

The critical insight from Booth's *The Rhetoric of Fiction* (1961): every text implies a narrator — a "second self" that the author creates through consistent choices of person, register, and stance. When that narrator is stable, the reader trusts the voice and follows the argument. When the narrator shifts without signal, the reader loses track of who is speaking and must reconstruct the narrator's identity from each new sentence. An autonomous writer that cannot maintain voice consistency produces text that is technically correct but rhetorically unmoored: each sentence sounds like it was written by a different person, and the reader never settles into a relationship with the narrator.

Reference: Booth, W.C. (1961), *The Rhetoric of Fiction*. Applied to autonomous writing: voice consistency is the failure mode that looks like fluency — grammatically correct prose that doesn't sound like anyone in particular.

## writer cohesion — measuring whether sentences connect to their neighbors

Cohesion is the micro-structural glue that the other metrics leave open: does each sentence connect to its neighbors, or does the reader have to leap across gaps? A text can be coherent (the argument progresses logically at the macro level) while having weak cohesion (each step is a jump rather than a slide). A text can be readable (simple words, short sentences) while being disjointed — each sentence is easy to parse individually, but there are no threads connecting them. A text can have strong signal clarity (each stroke says something new) while poor cohesion between sentences means the reader never sees how the new idea relates to the last one.

Four signals capture cohesion in writing. **Transition density** measures the frequency of explicit connective words and phrases per hundred words: "however", "therefore", "moreover", "in contrast", "as a result". These tell the reader *how* the next sentence relates to the previous one — addition, contrast, cause, example, concession. **Referential continuity** measures the fraction of sentences that reach back to prior context through pronouns, demonstratives, and anaphoric expressions. A sentence with referential continuity picks up a thread; a sentence without it starts a new one. **Lexical chain strength** measures the average Jaccard similarity of content words between consecutive sentence pairs — do vocabulary threads persist across boundaries, or does each sentence introduce entirely new terminology? **Structural parallelism** measures the fraction of consecutive sentence pairs sharing the same syntactic opening pattern. Moderate parallelism aids readability through rhythmic echo; extreme parallelism suggests mechanical repetition.

The critical insight from Halliday & Hasan's *Cohesion in English* (1976): cohesion is not style — it is the texture that makes a text a text rather than a list of sentences. Their five types of cohesive ties (reference, substitution, ellipsis, conjunction, lexical cohesion) are the theoretical basis for this module's four signals. An autonomous writer that cannot maintain cohesion produces output that reads like shuffled index cards: each sentence may be individually well-formed, but the reader cannot follow the thread from one to the next.

Reference: Halliday, M.A.K. & Hasan, R. (1976), *Cohesion in English*. Applied to autonomous writing: cohesion is the failure mode that looks like completeness — every fact present, every sentence grammatical, yet nothing seems to flow into the next thing.
